Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → [LLM] macOS.Gaslight LaunchAgent persistence masquerading as com.apple.system.services.activity · [LLM] macOS.Gaslight self-staged standalone CPython 3.10.18 fetch (astral-sh python-build-standalone) · [LLM] macOS.Gaslight known-bad file hashes (Mach-O implant, BONZAI sibling, Python/bash stages) · File hash IOCs — endpoint file/process match
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking. → [LLM] macOS.Gaslight Telegram Bot API C2 polling from non-browser process · Beaconing — periodic outbound to small set of destinations
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage. → [LLM] macOS.Gaslight keychain theft + collected_data.zip staging · Infostealer — non-browser process accessing browser cookie/login DBs
macOS.Gaslight Telegram Bot API C2 polling from non-browser process AICommand & Control Medium
Hunts macOS endpoints resolving/connecting to api.telegram.org from a process that is not a known browser or the legitimate Telegram desktop client. macOS.Gaslight runs its C2 as a Telegram Bot API getUpdates polling loop, so sustained api.telegram.org traffic from an ad-hoc-signed Rust binary is the C2 fingerprint. Rationale: Anchored on the verbatim getUpdates Telegram Bot API C2 (code block 2) plus the api.telegram.org endpoint; non-browser/non-Telegram initiating process is the differentiator. Cross-checked against SentinelLABS, Infosecurity Magazine and the PXA/Ghost-in-the-Zip Telegram-C2 reporting. Cross-checked against: • https://www.infosecurity-magazine.com/news/macos-gaslight-rust-backdoor/ • https://www.sentinelone.com/labs/ghost-in-the-zip-new-pxa-stealer-and-its-telegram-powered-ecosystem/
ATT&CKT1102.002 T1071.001
Data sourcesNetwork_Resolution.DNS
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate Telegram Desktop client installed under a non-standard process name
  • Developer/CI tooling using a Telegram bot for build notifications
  • Marketing or alerting scripts (python/node) posting to a Telegram bot
  • Browser extensions or Electron apps embedding Telegram web
  • Security tooling that itself proxies Telegram traffic
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessFolderPath !startswith "/Applications/Telegram.app"
Response runbook
1) Triage: identify InitiatingProcessFileName/SHA256 and folder path; confirm it is not Telegram Desktop or a sanctioned bot integration, and check if the binary is ad-hoc signed in an unusual path (e.g. user temp/Application Support). 2) Contain: isolate the host in MDE/Falcon, kill the polling process, and block the resolved IPs at egress. 3) Hunt: pivot on the same SHA256/parent across the fleet, and look for login.keychain-db copies, collected_data.zip, and the com.apple.system.services.activity LaunchAgent on the same host.
DeviceNetworkEvents

macOS.Gaslight keychain theft + collected_data.zip staging AIActions on Objectives High
Detects the macOS.Gaslight Python stealer copying login.keychain-db and archiving harvested data to collected_data.zip prior to Telegram exfiltration. The collected_data.zip filename and a login.keychain-db copy by a non-Apple process are high-fidelity collection indicators. Rationale: Uses the article's exact collection artifacts: a raw copy of login.keychain-db and the temp/collected_data.zip archive. SentinelLABS notes AMOS/early Rust macOS stealers harvest the same login.keychain-db, and Jamf documents DumpKeychain()-style theft, so the zip filename is the novel high-fidelity anchor. Cross-checked against: • https://www.jamf.com/blog/pyinstaller-malware-jamf-threat-labs/ • https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/security/blog/2026/02/02/infostealers-without-borders-macos-python-stealers-and-platform-abuse/

Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Time Machine / backupd legitimately reading login.keychain-db
  • Keychain First Aid or migration assistant copying the keychain during OS upgrade
  • Admin manually backing up ~/Library/Keychains before re-imaging
  • A user-created archive coincidentally named collected_data.zip (rare)
  • MDM agents snapshotting user profiles
Suppression filter

Response runbook
1) Triage: identify the InitiatingProcess copying login.keychain-db / writing collected_data.zip — legitimate copies come from backupd/securityd, not a user-temp binary. 2) Contain: isolate host, kill the process, and force-rotate any credentials/secrets stored in that user's keychain (SSO, VPN, cloud, SSH passphrases). 3) Hunt: look for the collected_data.zip being uploaded to api.telegram.org, the com.apple.system.services.activity LaunchAgent, and the same parent binary's SHA256 elsewhere.

Catches the implant establishing a persistent foothold. Rationale: Anchored on the exact LaunchAgent Label IOC from Table 2 (com.apple.system.services.activity) — there is no genuine Apple LaunchAgent by that name, so any plist bearing it is the implant. Consistent with SentinelLABS BlueNoroff/RustBucket macOS LaunchAgent persistence tradecraft.   • Internal MDM/config-profile deployment pushing a custom-labeled agent (would not impersonate com.apple.*)
  • Security product creating its own LaunchDaemon under a vendor label

ATT&CKT1204.002 T1027
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes Endpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Hash collision is cryptographically implausible — effectively zero benign matches
  • Security researcher detonating the sample in a sanctioned analysis VM
  • Threat-intel platform re-scanning the artifact during enrichment
  • EDR's own quarantine/restore re-touching the file (Evidence=File)

Beaconing — periodic outbound to small set of destinations Command & Control Medium
C2 channel detection via inter-beacon-time stddev / fan-out to single dest.
ATT&CKT1071.001 T1071.004
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic DeviceNetworkEvents
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where RemoteIPType == "Public" and ActionType == "ConnectionSuccess"
| project DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort, Timestamp
| sort by DeviceName asc, RemoteIP asc, RemotePort asc, Timestamp asc
| extend prev_dev = prev(DeviceName, 1), prev_ip = prev(RemoteIP, 1),
         prev_port = prev(RemotePort, 1), prev_ts = prev(Timestamp, 1)
| where DeviceName == prev_dev and RemoteIP == prev_ip and RemotePort == prev_port
| extend delta_sec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, prev_ts)
| summarize conn_count = count(), avg_delta = avg(delta_sec), stdev_delta = stdev(delta_sec)
    by DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort
| where conn_count > 30 and avg_delta between (30.0 .. 600.0) and stdev_delta < 5.0
| order by conn_count desc
ImNetworkSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(2h)
| where DvcAction != "Block"
| where ipv4_is_private(DstIpAddr) == false
| summarize ConnCount = count(),
            DistinctMinutes = dcount(bin(TimeGenerated, 1m))
            by DvcHostname, DstIpAddr, DstPortNumber
| where ConnCount > 50 and DistinctMinutes > 30   // sustained, not bursty
| order by ConnCount desc

Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count, values(All_Traffic.dest_port) AS ports
    from datamodel=Network_Traffic.All_Traffic
    where All_Traffic.action="allowed" AND All_Traffic.dest_category!="internal"
    by _time span=10s, All_Traffic.src, All_Traffic.dest
| `drop_dm_object_name(All_Traffic)`
| streamstats current=f last(_time) AS prev_time by src, dest
| eval delta = _time - prev_time
| stats avg(delta) AS avg_delta stdev(delta) AS sd_delta count by src, dest
| where count > 30 AND sd_delta < 5 AND avg_delta>=30 AND avg_delta<=600
| sort - count

    from datamodel=Network_Traffic.All_Traffic

    by _time span=10s, All_Traffic.src, All_Traffic.dest
| `drop_dm_object_name(All_Traffic)`
| streamstats current=f last(_time) AS prev_time by src, dest
| eval delta = _time - prev_time
| stats avg(delta) AS avg_delta stdev(delta) AS sd_delta count by src, dest
| where count > 30 AND sd_delta < 5 AND avg_delta>=30 AND avg_delta<=600

// Pre-filter each table BEFORE the union — the engine cannot push

                  Cmd = InitiatingProcessCommandLine, Source = "DeviceFileEvents" ),
    ( DeviceProcessEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(7d)
        | where AccountName !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ ("6328567511d88fdc2ae0939c5ef17b7a63d2a833881900de018a4f12f4982525", "77b4fd46994992f0e57302cfe76ed23c0d90101381d2b89fc2ddf5c4536e77ca", "baabf249c77bc54c54ab0e66e15af798bd28aa5b4683554456a8b73ab8741239", "b3c56d689414343589f38394d19ba2fe9a518133281200faa0556ba4e4136394") or SHA1 in~ ("6328567511d88fdc2ae0939c5ef17b7a63d2a833881900de018a4f12f4982525", "77b4fd46994992f0e57302cfe76ed23c0d90101381d2b89fc2ddf5c4536e77ca", "baabf249c77bc54c54ab0e66e15af798bd28aa5b4683554456a8b73ab8741239", "b3c56d689414343589f38394d19ba2fe9a518133281200faa0556ba4e4136394") or MD5 in~ ("6328567511d88fdc2ae0939c5ef17b7a63d2a833881900de018a4f12f4982525", "77b4fd46994992f0e57302cfe76ed23c0d90101381d2b89fc2ddf5c4536e77ca", "baabf249c77bc54c54ab0e66e15af798bd28aa5b4683554456a8b73ab8741239", "b3c56d689414343589f38394d19ba2fe9a518133281200faa0556ba4e4136394")

// Pre-filter both ASIM tables before the union — same pattern as the
// Defender version so the engine can push hash predicates per-side.
let BadHashes = dynamic(["6328567511d88fdc2ae0939c5ef17b7a63d2a833881900de018a4f12f4982525", "77b4fd46994992f0e57302cfe76ed23c0d90101381d2b89fc2ddf5c4536e77ca", "baabf249c77bc54c54ab0e66e15af798bd28aa5b4683554456a8b73ab8741239", "b3c56d689414343589f38394d19ba2fe9a518133281200faa0556ba4e4136394"]);
union
    ( ImFileEvent
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where TargetFileSHA256 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileSHA1 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileMD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType,
                  TargetFileName, TargetFilePath, TargetFileSHA256,
                  Cmd = ActingProcessName, Source = "ImFileEvent" ),
    ( ImProcessCreate
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ (BadHashes) or SHA1 in~ (BadHashes) or MD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileName = TargetProcessName, TargetFilePath = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileSHA256 = SHA256,
                  Cmd = TargetProcessCommandLine, Source = "ImProcessCreate" )
| order by TimeGenerated desc

motionEye Partial Authentication Bypass: Unauthenticated Admin Credential Theft via Path Traversal

SHA17b7d55439abccf4a…

Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → [LLM] motionEye unauthenticated path traversal read of motion.conf / system secrets · [LLM] motionEye traversal read followed by admin config-hook write from same source · Article-specific behavioural hunt — [GHSA / CRITICAL] GHSA-phv5-334h-mxcw: motionEye Partial Authentication Bypass:
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → File hash IOCs — endpoint file/process match
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage. → [LLM] motionEye RCE: motion daemon spawns shell via injected command_*_exec hook
motionEye unauthenticated path traversal read of motion.conf / system secrets AIExploitation High
Hunts the CVE-chain arbitrary-file-read: unauthenticated GET requests to motionEye's movie/picture playback/download/preview handlers whose filename segment is an absolute path (e.g. /etc/motioneye/motion.conf, /etc/passwd, /etc/shadow). This is article-specific because the vulnerable handlers override Tornado's path validation and os.path.join drops target_dir on absolute paths. Rationale: Uses the exact vulnerable URL handler segments (/playback/, /download/, /preview/) from movie_playback.py combined with absolute-path targets named in the PoC (/etc/motioneye/motion.conf, /etc/passwd, /etc/shadow). A GET to a media handler that resolves an absolute system path is not a normal media request, giving very low FP. Cross-checked the GHSA advisory body and motionEye source references cited within it. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-phv5-334h-mxcw • https://github.com/motioneye-project/motioneye
ATT&CKT1190 T1083 T1552.001
Data sourcesWeb.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Vulnerability scanner (Nuclei/Nessus) probing the disclosed motionEye path-traversal template
  • Internal pentest / red-team validation of the same CVE
  • A security researcher re-testing after patching to 0.44.0
  • Backup or monitoring tooling that legitimately requests recorded media by absolute NAS mount path (rare, verify the path is media not /etc)
Suppression filter
| where HttpRequestHeaderUserAgent !has_any ("Nuclei","nessus","nmap","zgrab")
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm the source IP is external/untrusted and that the requested path resolved to a config/secret file rather than a media file; check HTTP status 200 vs 404 to see if the read succeeded. 2) Contain: block the source IP at the proxy/WAF, take the motionEye instance off the public internet, and rotate the @admin_password (its SHA-1 hash is now assumed stolen and usable directly as a signing key). 3) Hunt: pivot on the same source IP for subsequent POST /config/<id>/set requests (hash-reuse admin takeover) and for motion-daemon child processes (RCE via command_notifications_exec).
ImWebSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where HttpMethod == "GET"
| where Url has_any ("/playback/","/download/","/preview/")
| where Url has_any ("motion.conf","/etc/passwd","/etc/shadow","/etc/","/root/",".ssh")
| project TimeGenerated, SrcIpAddr, DstHostname, HttpMethod, Url, HttpStatusCode, HttpRequestHeaderUserAgent
| order by TimeGenerated desc

motionEye RCE: motion daemon spawns shell via injected command_*_exec hook AIActions on Objectives Medium
Hunts post-compromise RCE where a stolen admin hash is used to set command_notifications_exec / command_storage_exec, causing the motion daemon to spawn shell/utility children (e.g. sh -c 'touch /tmp/pwned'). Article-specific because RCE lands as a child of the 'motion' process via the event-hook mechanism, not a web shell. Rationale: Anchors on the specific parent process 'motion' and the injected-command interpreters/utilities from the PoC (command_notifications_exec: 'touch /tmp/pwned'). Medium confidence/FP because the advisory explicitly notes hook-driven command execution is a legitimate feature — so this is a hunting pivot best correlated with UC3's config-write signal. Cross-checked GHSA advisory (Impact/PoC sections). Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-phv5-334h-mxcw
ATT&CKT1059.004 T1190
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • User has legitimately configured a notification/storage command hook (upload to NAS, ffmpeg post-processing, pushover/curl notification)
  • motion daemon running a benign cleanup script on event end
  • Home-automation integration invoking curl/wget on motion detection
  • Admin-authored maintenance hook that shells out
Suppression filter
| where ProcessCommandLine !has_any ("ffmpeg","/usr/local/bin/notify","pushover","mqtt")
Response runbook
1) Triage: pull ProcessCommandLine of the child — a configured hook (curl to a known notify URL, ffmpeg) is benign; an unexpected reverse shell, /tmp write, or credential-dump command is malicious. Cross-reference the host against UC1/UC3 traversal+config-write hits. 2) Contain: kill the offending process tree, isolate the host, and revert motion.conf command_*_exec / web_hook_storage_url to a known-good baseline. 3) Hunt: search the same host for new listeners, cron/systemd persistence, and SSH key additions dropped by the child process; review all recent /config/<id>/set POSTs.

motionEye traversal read followed by admin config-hook write from same source AIExploitation High
Correlates the two halves of the full chain from one source IP: an unauthenticated path-traversal GET that reads motion.conf/secrets, followed within a short window by a POST /config/<id>/set (hash-reuse admin takeover writing command_*_exec hooks). This full read-then-write sequence is the highest-fidelity signal that the auth bypass succeeded, not just was probed. Rationale: Ties the article's Step 1 (traversal read of motion.conf) to Step 3 (POST /config/<id>/set) by shared source IP inside a 1h window — a sequence no benign client produces. Sigma omitted because this is a cross-request time-window correlation, not a single-event shape. Switched off CommonSecurityLog (which lacks RequestMethod/config-write columns cleanly) onto ASIM ImWebSession canonical columns (HttpMethod/Url/SrcIpAddr). Cross-checked the GHSA PoC steps. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-phv5-334h-mxcw
ATT&CKT1190 T1059.004 T1552.001
Data sourcesWeb.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate admin who first browses recorded footage then edits camera settings from the same IP (verify the GET was an absolute /etc path, not a media file)
  • Automated config-management tooling that both reads media paths and pushes config
  • Vulnerability scanner that chains the full PoC during an authorized assessment
  • NAT gateway aggregating an admin session and unrelated viewer traffic behind one source IP
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm the GET actually targeted an absolute system path (motion.conf/etc) and returned 200, and inspect the POST body/parameters for command_notifications_exec, command_storage_exec, or web_hook_storage_url being set. 2) Contain: block the source IP, take motionEye off the internet, rotate @admin_password, and revert motion.conf to a clean baseline removing any injected command hooks. 3) Hunt: on the motionEye host, run UC2 to find motion-daemon child shells, then check for persistence (cron, systemd, SSH keys) and any secondary outbound C2 from the box.
let Window = 1h;
let Reads = ImWebSession
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
    | where HttpMethod == "GET"
    | where Url has_any ("/playback/","/download/","/preview/")
    | where Url has_any ("motion.conf","/etc/","/etc/shadow","/etc/passwd")
    | project ReadTime = TimeGenerated, SrcIpAddr, ReadUrl = Url;

| where HttpMethod == "POST"
| where Url matches regex @"/config/\d+/set"
| join kind=inner Reads on SrcIpAddr
| where TimeGenerated between (ReadTime .. ReadTime + Window)
| project ReadTime, WriteTime = TimeGenerated, SrcIpAddr, ReadUrl, WriteUrl = Url
| order by WriteTime desc

// Pre-filter each table BEFORE the union — the engine cannot push
// predicates across `union`, and DeviceFileEvents lacks ProcessCommandLine
// (Initiating only) so column-projection has to differ per side.
union
    ( DeviceFileEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(7d)
        | where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ ("7b7d55439abccf4ae83047c1af2707e6eb6664db") or SHA1 in~ ("7b7d55439abccf4ae83047c1af2707e6eb6664db") or MD5 in~ ("7b7d55439abccf4ae83047c1af2707e6eb6664db")
        | project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256,
                  Cmd = InitiatingProcessCommandLine, Source = "DeviceFileEvents" ),
    ( DeviceProcessEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(7d)
        | where AccountName !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ ("7b7d55439abccf4ae83047c1af2707e6eb6664db") or SHA1 in~ ("7b7d55439abccf4ae83047c1af2707e6eb6664db") or MD5 in~ ("7b7d55439abccf4ae83047c1af2707e6eb6664db")
        | project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256,
                  Cmd = ProcessCommandLine, Source = "DeviceProcessEvents" )
| order by Timestamp desc

// Pre-filter both ASIM tables before the union — same pattern as the
// Defender version so the engine can push hash predicates per-side.
let BadHashes = dynamic(["7b7d55439abccf4ae83047c1af2707e6eb6664db"]);
union
    ( ImFileEvent
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where TargetFileSHA256 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileSHA1 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileMD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType,
                  TargetFileName, TargetFilePath, TargetFileSHA256,
                  Cmd = ActingProcessName, Source = "ImFileEvent" ),
    ( ImProcessCreate
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ (BadHashes) or SHA1 in~ (BadHashes) or MD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileName = TargetProcessName, TargetFilePath = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileSHA256 = SHA256,
                  Cmd = TargetProcessCommandLine, Source = "ImProcessCreate" )
| order by TimeGenerated desc

motionEye picture-download LFI via absolute path (/picture/<id>/download/<abs>) AIExploitation High
Hunts the motionEye arbitrary-file-read primitive (GHSA-qxvg-h7q2-hcxh) where get_media_content() only blocks '..' but honours absolute paths, so /picture/<id>/download/%2Fetc%2Fmotioneye%2Fmotion.conf leaks the @admin_password SHA1 hash. Targets the specific download endpoint carrying an absolute/sensitive path, which never appears in legitimate media downloads that use relative date-folder paths. Rationale: Uses the exact vulnerable endpoint shape /picture/<id>/download/ combined with absolute-path / URL-encoded-slash markers and the specific sensitive target /etc/motioneye/motion.conf named in the advisory as holding @admin_password. Legit downloads never pass an absolute path, so the combination is near-zero-FP. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-qxvg-h7q2-hcxh • https://advisories.gitlab.com/pypi/motioneye/GHSA-qxvg-h7q2-hcxh/ • https://github.com/motioneye-project/motioneye/releases
ATT&CKT1190 T1083 T1552.001
Data sourcesWeb.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate camera media download using a relative date path that coincidentally contains the word 'motion' (unlikely to include /etc/ or an absolute path)
  • Vulnerability scanners (Nessus/Nuclei) probing the motionEye host — true detection of scanning, not a benign FP
  • Security-team validation of the patch on 0.44.0 (path now blocked, request may still be logged)
  • Backup tooling that fetches media via the API using absolute host paths (rare, should be allow-listed by source IP)
Suppression filter
| where not (HttpStatusCode in ("404","403") and Url !contains "/etc/")
Response runbook
1) TRIAGE: Confirm the request returned HTTP 200 and the path targeted /etc/motioneye/motion.conf or /etc/passwd; pull the source IP and check whether it also hit /config/restore and /action/* shortly after (the full chain). 2) CONTAIN: Block the source IP at the proxy/WAF, and rotate the motionEye admin/normal passwords immediately since the SHA1 hash enables pass-the-hash admin. Take the instance offline if it is <0.44.0. 3) HUNT: Search host process/file telemetry for any process executing from /etc/motioneye/ and for tar/python writing non-.conf files there (see companion UCs).

| where Url contains "/download//" or Url contains "/download/%2f" or Url contains "/etc/motioneye" or Url contains "/etc/passwd" or Url contains "/etc/shadow" or Url contains "motion.conf"
| project TimeGenerated, SrcIpAddr, DstHostname, HttpStatusCode, UserAgent = HttpRequestHeaderUserAgent, Url
| order by TimeGenerated desc

motionEye unsafe restore drops non-config file into CONF_PATH (/etc/motioneye) AIInstallation Medium
Hunts the unsafe config-restore step (config.py restore() using 'tar zxC CONF_PATH' without sanitisation) that extracts an attacker tarball into /etc/motioneye/, dropping an executable camera-action script (e.g. lock_1). Filters out the four legitimate restore artefacts confirmed by the 0.44.0 fix (motion.conf, camera-*.conf, prefs.json, mask-*.pgm), leaving only the injected payload. Rationale: Anchors on CONF_PATH (/etc/motioneye/) and the 'tar zxC' restore mechanism from config.py, and derives the exclusion list (.conf/.json/.pgm) directly from the 0.44.0 fix note that legitimate restore only handles motion.conf, camera-*.conf, prefs.json and mask-*.pgm — so any other file written there by tar/python is the injected action script. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-qxvg-h7q2-hcxh • https://advisories.gitlab.com/pypi/motioneye/GHSA-qxvg-h7q2-hcxh/ • https://github.com/motioneye-project/motioneye/releases
ATT&CKT1105 T1190
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Admin performing a genuine backup restore that legitimately contains a custom action script (rare; still worth reviewing)
  • Package upgrade to 0.44.0 rewriting files under /etc/motioneye/
  • Manual sysadmin editing/creating an action script via an editor (Image would be vim/nano, not tar/python — largely excluded by the process filter)
  • Configuration-management tooling (Ansible/Puppet) deploying camera action scripts to CONF_PATH
Suppression filter
| where not (InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("dpkg","apt","rpm") or InitiatingProcessCommandLine has "upgrade")
Response runbook
1) TRIAGE: Identify the file written (name and hash), read its contents — an injected action script is a small executable/shell payload, not a .conf. Check whether a /config/restore POST preceded it and whether /action/<id>/<action> was called after. 2) CONTAIN: Quarantine/delete the dropped file, kill any child process spawned from /etc/motioneye/, and isolate the host if the file executed. 3) HUNT: Pivot on the SHA256 across the fleet, review web logs for the LFI (companion UC) and restore requests, and rotate motionEye credentials.

| order by Timestamp desc

motionEye RCE: process executed from CONF_PATH (/etc/motioneye) via action handler AIExploitation High
Hunts the terminal RCE of GHSA-qxvg-h7q2-hcxh where the unauthenticated /action/<id>/<action> handler calls subprocess.Popen on '<action>_<camera_id>' living in CONF_PATH, so a payload executes directly from /etc/motioneye/. A configuration directory should never be a process image location, making any execution from that path high-fidelity. Rationale: Directly targets the subprocess.Popen execution of '<action>_<camera_id>' from CONF_PATH described in action.py; executing a process from /etc/motioneye/ has no legitimate cause, so a single-clause path filter is both precise and low-FP, warranting alerting tier. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-qxvg-h7q2-hcxh • https://advisories.gitlab.com/pypi/motioneye/GHSA-qxvg-h7q2-hcxh/ • https://github.com/motioneye-project/motioneye/releases

Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Administrator legitimately configured a custom camera action script that motionEye executes on a real event (lock/unlock/light_on) — verify the script's provenance and web-request trigger
  • Security testing/validation of the vulnerability in a lab
  • Backup/restore tooling temporarily executing helper scripts from the config dir (rare)
  • A monitoring agent mistakenly configured to run from /etc/motioneye/ (misconfiguration, still worth flagging)
Response runbook
1) TRIAGE: Capture the process image, hash, command line and parent (should be the motionEye python/meyectl process); read the on-disk file at FolderPath. Correlate with a preceding unauthenticated POST /action/<id>/<action> in web logs. 2) CONTAIN: Kill the process, isolate the host, delete the injected script from /etc/motioneye/, and rotate motionEye admin/normal credentials (pass-the-hash exposure). 3) HUNT: Sweep the SHA256 fleet-wide, review outbound connections from the spawned process (reverse shell / C2), and confirm the instance is upgraded to motionEye >= 0.44.0.

Budibase published-app query endpoint hit with NoSQL operator-injection body (CVE-2026-54350) AIExploitation High
Hunts unauthenticated exploitation of CVE-2026-54350: a POST to Budibase's /api/v2/queries/:queryId endpoint whose JSON body carries injected Mongo/CouchDB/Elasticsearch operator keys ($exists, $where, $ne, $regex, $comment) broken out of a builder parameter via JSON metacharacters. A '$'-prefixed operator token in a public form parameter is never legitimate user input, so this is high-fidelity for the exact injection primitive described in the advisory. Rationale: Uses the advisory's exact endpoint (POST /api/v2/queries/:queryId) plus the injected NoSQL operator tokens ($exists/$comment seen in the PoC, generalised to the Mongo/Mango operator set). A '$'-operator key inside a public query parameter cannot occur in legitimate Budibase form input, giving very low FP. Corroborated against IONIX, GitLab Advisory DB, Kodem, and TheHackerWire. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-8qv3-p479-cj62 • https://www.ionix.io/threat-center/cve-2026-54350/ • https://advisories.gitlab.com/npm/@budibase/server/CVE-2026-54350/ • https://www.kodemsecurity.com/cve-archive/cve-2026-54350
ATT&CKT1190
Data sourcesWeb
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Authorized pentest / vulnerability scanner (Nuclei, Burp) firing the CVE-2026-54350 template against the app
  • A public form where a user legitimately types a support string containing the literal text '$where' or '$exists' as free text
  • Post-patch developer validation replaying the PoC in a staging environment
  • WAF re-ingestion / SIEM replay double-counting the same request
  • Security researcher responsibly probing the deployment
Suppression filter
| where RequestContext matches regex @'(?i)\\"?,?\\"?\$(exists|where|ne|gt|gte|lt|lte|in|nin|regex|comment|or|and)\\"\s*:'
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm the RequestURL is /api/v2/queries/<id>, the source is external/unauthenticated, and the body contains a '$'-prefixed operator inside the parameters object (not free-text). Pull the DestinationServiceName to identify which Budibase instance and which datasource (Mongo/Couch/ES/DynamoDB/REST) backs that query. 2) Contain: block the source IP at the WAF, and until upgrade to >=3.39.12, set the affected query's role away from PUBLIC and re-publish, or take the published app offline. 3) Hunt: pivot on the source IP across all /api/v2/queries calls; check for oversized 200 responses (mass read — see companion UC) and for response bodies containing matchedCount/modifiedCount (mass write via updateMany). Review the backing DB for unexpected document modifications.
CommonSecurityLog
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where RequestMethod == "POST"
| where RequestURL has "/api/v2/queries/"
| where RequestContext has_any ("$exists","$where","$regex","$comment","$ne","$gt","$gte","$lt","$lte","$in","$nin","$or","$and")
    or Message has_any ("$exists","$where","$regex","$comment","$ne","$gt","$gte","$lt","$lte","$in","$nin","$or","$and")
| project TimeGenerated, SourceIP, DestinationServiceName, RequestURL, RequestMethod, RequestContext, Message, RequestClientApplication, DeviceVendor, DeviceProduct
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Anonymous bulk document read from Budibase PUBLIC query (oversized response, CVE-2026-54350) AIActions on Objectives Medium
Hunts the data-exfiltration outcome of CVE-2026-54350: a POST to /api/v2/queries/:queryId that returns HTTP 200 with a response body far larger than the single-record result a public form query is designed to return, indicating the injected {$exists:true} filter dumped the entire backing collection. Complements the request-signature UC by catching successful mass reads even when body inspection is unavailable. Rationale: A published single-record public query (the canonical Budibase public-form use case) returns a small body; the CVE-2026-54350 injection collapses the filter to {$exists:true} and returns the full collection, ballooning the 200-response size. The 100KB threshold is an empirical starting point (tune per app — a single JSON record is typically low single-digit KB). Defender/Datadog/Falcon/CloudWatch left blank: none carry HTTP response-size for a self-hosted Node app. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-8qv3-p479-cj62 • https://www.ionix.io/threat-center/cve-2026-54350/ • https://www.thehackerwire.com/budibase-unauthenticated-critical-data-exposure-and-manipulation-cve-2026-54350/
ATT&CKT1190 T1213
Data sourcesWeb
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • A legitimately large public query (e.g. a public directory or catalog listing) that is designed to return many rows
  • A report/export-style PUBLIC query returning bulk JSON by design
  • Verbose documents (large embedded blobs) inflating a normal single-record response above the threshold
  • Load testing or synthetic monitoring hitting the endpoint
  • Response-size inflation from disabled compression on the proxy
Response runbook
1) Triage: identify the queryId and confirm whether its builder-intended result is single-record (public form) vs bulk (listing). Compare the flagged max_out against the historical baseline for that same uri_path — a sudden jump on a normally-small query is the signal. 2) Contain: block the source IP and change the query role off PUBLIC until the instance is upgraded to >=3.39.12. 3) Hunt: correlate the source IP with the companion request-signature UC to confirm operator injection in the body; enumerate every distinct queryId that same IP touched; assess which sensitive fields (password_hash, secret, api_token, mfa_secret) live in the exposed collection to scope the breach.

| order by HttpResponseBodyBytes desc

Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → [LLM] Gogs CVE-2026-52813: path-traversal org/repo name in HTTP request targeting gogs data dir · [LLM] Gogs Git hook (hooks/update) shell execution under tmp worktree — CVE-2026-52813 RCE · Article-specific behavioural hunt — [GHSA / CRITICAL] CVE-2026-52813: Gogs has Path Traversal in organization name t
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → [LLM] Malicious Git hook file written under Gogs tmp worktree — CVE-2026-52813 payload plant · File hash IOCs — endpoint file/process match
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.
Gogs CVE-2026-52813: path-traversal org/repo name in HTTP request targeting gogs data dir AIExploitation Medium
Hunts inbound Gogs web requests whose URL carries repeated ../ traversal sequences resolving into the Gogs data directory (data/gogs/data/tmp/local-r), the fingerprint of the CVE-2026-52813 nested-repo abuse. This is the initial exploit step where an attacker references a repository/org written outside the intended repositories path. Rationale: Anchors on the CVE-2026-52813-specific traversal target string 'data/gogs/data/tmp/local-r' (the local worktree the exploit abuses) rather than a generic '../', keeping FPs to real exploit traffic vs. broad scanner noise; corroborated by GitLab GLAD and The Hacker News. Cross-checked against: • https://advisories.gitlab.com/golang/gogs.io/gogs/CVE-2026-52813/ • https://thehackernews.com/2026/05/critical-gogs-rce-vulnerability-lets.html • https://ccb.belgium.be/advisories/warning-multiple-vulnerabilities-gogs-allow-remote-code-execution-patch-immediately
ATT&CKT1190
Data sourcesWeb
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Vulnerability scanners (Nuclei/Nessus/Burp) probing generic ../ traversal against the Gogs host — usually 400/404 status
  • Authorized pentest or red-team validation of CVE-2026-52813
  • Legitimate CI/CD tooling that URL-encodes repo paths oddly (rare, would not contain 'data/gogs/data/tmp/local-r')
  • WAF that logs the pre-decode raw URI, double-counting one request
  • Backup/mirroring agents crawling Gogs raw endpoints
Suppression filter
| where status="200" OR status="201" OR status="302"
Response runbook
1) Triage: pull all requests from Web.src for the alert window; confirm the URL literally references 'data/gogs/data/tmp/local-r' and check response status — a 200/201 means the traversal resolved and a repo/hook was likely written. 2) Contain: block the source IP at the proxy, disable the offending Gogs user account, and (if unpatched) restrict org creation / disable self-registration; snapshot /data/gogs/data/tmp for forensics. 3) Hunt: pivot to UC2/UC3 on the Gogs host for a hooks/update file write or a git-hook shell execution under /data/gogs/data/tmp/local-r, and verify the instance is upgraded to >= 0.14.3.
ImWebSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where Url has "data/gogs" and (Url contains "../" or Url contains "..%2f")
| where Url has "local-r" or Url contains "../../../../"
| project TimeGenerated, SrcIpAddr, HttpHostName, HttpMethod, Url, HttpStatusCode, HttpRequestHeaderUserAgent
| sort by TimeGenerated desc

Gogs Git hook (hooks/update) shell execution under tmp worktree — CVE-2026-52813 RCE AIExploitation High
Hunts execution of a Git update hook located under the Gogs local worktree path /data/gogs/data/tmp/local-r, which is where CVE-2026-52813 plants an attacker-controlled hooks/update script. Legitimate Gogs hooks live under gogs-repositories, never under the tmp/local-r worktree, so execution from this path is the RCE firing as the git user. Rationale: Uses the two immutable path components of the CVE-2026-52813 exploit — the git-hook name 'hooks/update' and the tmp worktree 'data/gogs/data/tmp/local-r' — whose combination has no legitimate cause (real hooks live under gogs-repositories), yielding a near-zero-FP RCE signal as the git user. Cross-checked against: • https://advisories.gitlab.com/golang/gogs.io/gogs/CVE-2026-52813/ • https://github.com/gogs/gogs/releases/tag/v0.14.3
ATT&CKT1059.004 T1190
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Authorized pentest reproducing CVE-2026-52813 on a test Gogs instance
  • A DFIR/backup tool copying repo trees that momentarily executes hooks (rare on Linux)
  • Custom admin scripts intentionally placing hooks under a non-standard path (misconfiguration, not attack)
  • Sandbox/malware-analysis host detonating the public PoC
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
Response runbook
1) Triage: capture the full process tree — the update-hook process, its git/gogs parent, and any child shell — plus the running user (expect uid git). Read the hook script contents from /data/gogs/data/tmp/local-r/.../hooks/update. 2) Contain: isolate the Gogs host, kill the git-user shell descendants, disable the attacker Gogs account, and preserve the tmp worktree and any dropped output files (e.g. 'pwned'). 3) Hunt: enumerate all orgs/repos with '../' in their names via Gogs DB/API, review web logs (UC1) for the initiating traversal request and source IP, and check for post-exploit persistence or outbound C2 from the git user; patch to >= 0.14.3.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where (FolderPath has "hooks/update" or ProcessCommandLine has "hooks/update")
| where (FolderPath has "/data/gogs/data/tmp/local-r/" or ProcessCommandLine has "/data/gogs/data/tmp/local-r/")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FolderPath, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessFolderPath, InitiatingProcessCommandLine
| order by Timestamp desc
ImProcessCreate
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where (ActingProcessCommandLine has "hooks/update" or TargetProcessCommandLine has "hooks/update" or TargetProcessName has "hooks/update")
| where (ActingProcessCommandLine has "/data/gogs/data/tmp/local-r/" or TargetProcessCommandLine has "/data/gogs/data/tmp/local-r/" or TargetProcessName has "/data/gogs/data/tmp/local-r/")
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ParentProcessName, ActingProcessName, TargetProcessName, TargetProcessCommandLine
| sort by TimeGenerated desc

Malicious Git hook file written under Gogs tmp worktree — CVE-2026-52813 payload plant AIInstallation High
Hunts creation of a Git server-side hook file (update / pre-receive / post-receive) inside the Gogs local worktree path /data/gogs/data/tmp/local-r, the exact location CVE-2026-52813 abuses to plant an attacker-controlled hook via path traversal. File writes of executable hooks here precede the RCE and give earlier warning than hook execution. Rationale: Targets the write of a named Git hook (update/pre-receive/etc.) under the traversal-specific 'data/gogs/data/tmp/local-r' worktree — a path where Gogs never legitimately stores executable hooks — catching the CVE-2026-52813 payload one step before execution. Cross-checked against: • https://advisories.gitlab.com/golang/gogs.io/gogs/CVE-2026-52813/ • https://github.com/gogs/gogs/releases/tag/v0.14.3
ATT&CKT1546 T1059.004
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Authorized pentest reproducing the CVE-2026-52813 PoC
  • Gogs internal maintenance that transiently stages hooks (should be under gogs-repositories, not tmp/local-r)
  • Admin manually installing custom server-side hooks in a non-standard directory (misconfiguration)
  • Malware-analysis detonation of the public PoC script
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
Response runbook
1) Triage: read the newly written hook file under /data/gogs/data/tmp/local-r/.../hooks/ and note the writing process/user; a hook body containing shell commands (e.g. base64 -d | bash, or a redirect to a file like 'pwned') confirms malicious intent. 2) Contain: quarantine/remove the hook file, isolate the host if a shell has already spawned, and disable the responsible Gogs account. 3) Hunt: correlate to UC2 (hook execution) and UC1 (the traversal web request + source IP), search all repos/orgs for '../' names, and confirm the instance is patched to >= 0.14.3 with self-registration disabled.
DeviceFileEvents

| project Timestamp, DeviceName, FileName, FolderPath, InitiatingProcessAccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine
| order by Timestamp desc

| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine, TargetFilePath, TargetFileName
| sort by TimeGenerated desc

File hash IOCs — endpoint file/process match Installation High
Match SHA256/SHA1/MD5 named in the article against EDR file/process telemetry.
ATT&CKT1027
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem Endpoint.Processes DeviceFileEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Pre-filter each table BEFORE the union — the engine cannot push
// predicates across `union`, and DeviceFileEvents lacks ProcessCommandLine
// (Initiating only) so column-projection has to differ per side.
union
    ( DeviceFileEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(7d)
        | where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ ("d7571322a04a29476d4241406ed50bf7eef0a5b7", "4b72063338b27140f4439c524d98f2b9") or SHA1 in~ ("d7571322a04a29476d4241406ed50bf7eef0a5b7", "4b72063338b27140f4439c524d98f2b9") or MD5 in~ ("d7571322a04a29476d4241406ed50bf7eef0a5b7", "4b72063338b27140f4439c524d98f2b9")
        | project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256,
                  Cmd = InitiatingProcessCommandLine, Source = "DeviceFileEvents" ),
    ( DeviceProcessEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(7d)
        | where AccountName !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ ("d7571322a04a29476d4241406ed50bf7eef0a5b7", "4b72063338b27140f4439c524d98f2b9") or SHA1 in~ ("d7571322a04a29476d4241406ed50bf7eef0a5b7", "4b72063338b27140f4439c524d98f2b9") or MD5 in~ ("d7571322a04a29476d4241406ed50bf7eef0a5b7", "4b72063338b27140f4439c524d98f2b9")
        | project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256,
                  Cmd = ProcessCommandLine, Source = "DeviceProcessEvents" )
| order by Timestamp desc

// Pre-filter both ASIM tables before the union — same pattern as the
// Defender version so the engine can push hash predicates per-side.
let BadHashes = dynamic(["d7571322a04a29476d4241406ed50bf7eef0a5b7", "4b72063338b27140f4439c524d98f2b9"]);
union
    ( ImFileEvent
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where TargetFileSHA256 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileSHA1 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileMD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType,
                  TargetFileName, TargetFilePath, TargetFileSHA256,
                  Cmd = ActingProcessName, Source = "ImFileEvent" ),
    ( ImProcessCreate
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ (BadHashes) or SHA1 in~ (BadHashes) or MD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileName = TargetProcessName, TargetFilePath = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileSHA256 = SHA256,
                  Cmd = TargetProcessCommandLine, Source = "ImProcessCreate" )
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → [LLM] Gogs git post-receive/update hook spawning a shell or network tool (CVE-2026-52811 RCE trigger) · Article-specific behavioural hunt — [GHSA / CRITICAL] CVE-2026-52811: Gogs: UploadRepoFiles writes outside repo work
Installation. Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage. → Infostealer — non-browser process accessing browser cookie/login DBs
Gogs process writing to git server-side hook or authorized_keys (CVE-2026-52811 symlink write) AIInstallation Medium
Hunts the arbitrary-file-write primitive of CVE-2026-52811: the gogs web binary (running as the git UID) writing bytes into a repository's .git/hooks/post-receive (next-push RCE) or ~git/.ssh/authorized_keys (SSH foothold) because iox.CopyFile followed an attacker-committed parent directory symlink. Article-specific because it pins the writer to the 'gogs' process and the two exact sink files named in the advisory. Rationale: Pins the writer to process 'gogs' plus the two exact sink files (authorized_keys and hooks/post-receive) the advisory names as the impact of the symlink-followed CopyFile; second-sourced by the GitLab advisory and Wiz/Upwind write-ups of the same Gogs symlink-write family. Cross-checked against: • https://advisories.gitlab.com/golang/gogs.io/gogs/CVE-2026-52811/ • https://www.wiz.io/blog/wiz-research-gogs-cve-2025-8110-rce-exploit • https://nvd.nist.gov/vuln/detail/CVE-2026-52811
ATT&CKT1190 T1098.004
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Gogs writing the delegating hook stubs (pre-receive/update/post-receive) legitimately at repository creation
  • Gogs SSH key management rewriting ~git/.ssh/authorized_keys when users add or remove deploy/user keys via the UI
  • Administrator editing custom hooks through the Gogs repo settings UI
  • Repository import/mirror or backup-restore repopulating hook files
  • Config-management (Ansible/Puppet) re-templating the git service account's authorized_keys
Suppression filter
| where not (FolderPath has "/hooks/" and InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("create","init","migrate","mirror"))
Response runbook
1) TRIAGE: For a hook hit, diff the written hooks/* file against the gogs delegating stub (it should only exec `gogs hook ...`); any shell/curl/interpreter content = confirmed. For authorized_keys, check for entries lacking the `command="...gogs serv key-N...",no-port-forwarding` forced-command prefix. 2) CONTAIN: Isolate the Gogs host, revoke/rotate any non-gogs-managed keys in authorized_keys, restore the hook file, and suspend the two attacker repo-write accounts. 3) HUNT: Pivot on the repoID/temp path under AppDataPath/tmp/local-r for committed symlinks (mode-120000) pointing outside the repo, and correlate with UC2 (post-receive spawning a shell) and UC3 (interactive git-user SSH).
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName =~ "gogs"
| where (FileName =~ "authorized_keys" and FolderPath has "/.ssh/")
     or (FolderPath has "/hooks/" and FileName in~ ("post-receive","pre-receive","update","post-update"))
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, FileName, FolderPath, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, ActionType
| order by Timestamp desc
ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where ActingProcessName has "gogs"
| where TargetFilePath has "/.ssh/authorized_keys"
     or (TargetFilePath has "/hooks/" and (TargetFilePath endswith "/post-receive" or TargetFilePath endswith "/pre-receive" or TargetFilePath endswith "/update" or TargetFilePath endswith "/post-update"))
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine, TargetFilePath, DvcAction
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Gogs git post-receive/update hook spawning a shell or network tool (CVE-2026-52811 RCE trigger) AIExploitation High
Detects the RCE payoff of CVE-2026-52811: an overwritten server-side git hook (post-receive/pre-receive/update) firing on the next push and spawning an interpreter or download tool instead of the benign `gogs hook` delegator. This is the execution stage of the symlink-write chain. Rationale: The advisory names post-receive hook overwrite as the direct RCE vector; a git hook's only legitimate child on a Gogs box is the `gogs hook` delegator, so a hook parenting bash/curl/python is near-zero-FP and confirms exploitation. Cross-checked against: • https://advisories.gitlab.com/golang/gogs.io/gogs/CVE-2026-52811/ • https://www.upwind.io/feed/cve-2025-8110-gogs-rce-unpatched
ATT&CKT1059.004 T1546
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Custom legitimate hooks that intentionally run CI/notification scripts (curl to Slack/webhook) on push
  • Gitea/Gogs mirror or CI integration hooks invoking git-lfs or fetch helpers
  • Admin-authored post-receive deploy scripts that pull artifacts
  • Backup jobs invoking hooks during repo verification
Suppression filter
| where not (ProcessCommandLine has_any ("hooks.slack.com","/opt/ci/","gogs hook"))
Response runbook
1) TRIAGE: Read the hook file at the ParentImage path and the child ProcessCommandLine; benign Gogs hooks only exec `gogs hook <name>`. Any inline shell, base64, or outbound curl/wget = confirmed RCE. 2) CONTAIN: Isolate the host, kill the child process tree, restore the hook from a known-good repo template, and block the C2 destination seen in the child cmdline. 3) HUNT: Identify the pushing user and source IP from Gogs access logs, enumerate other repos owned by that account for planted symlinks/hooks, and sweep for UC1 file-write events preceding this execution.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("post-receive","pre-receive","update","post-update")
| where FileName in~ ("bash","sh","dash","ash","zsh","curl","wget","python","python3","perl","nc","ncat","socat")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, FileName, ProcessCommandLine, FolderPath
| order by Timestamp desc
ImProcessCreate
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("post-receive","pre-receive","update","post-update")
| where ActingProcessName has_any ("/bash","/sh","/dash","/ash","/zsh","/curl","/wget","/python","/perl","/nc","/ncat","/socat")
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ParentProcessName, ParentProcessCommandLine, ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Interactive shell for Gogs 'git' service account via SSH (CVE-2026-52811 authorized_keys foothold) AIInstallation High
Detects the SSH-foothold payoff of CVE-2026-52811: an attacker key implanted into ~git/.ssh/authorized_keys yields an interactive shell as the git service account, bypassing Gogs' forced `gogs serv` command. Normal Gogs SSH sessions never spawn bash/sh, so an sshd-parented shell under the git user is high-fidelity. Rationale: Gogs keys in authorized_keys always carry a forced `command="gogs serv ..."`, so SSH sessions for the git user only ever exec the gogs binary; an sshd-parented interactive shell for that account is the direct symptom of an unrestricted attacker key implanted via the CVE-2026-52811 write. Cross-checked against: • https://advisories.gitlab.com/golang/gogs.io/gogs/CVE-2026-52811/ • https://www.wiz.io/blog/wiz-research-gogs-cve-2025-8110-rce-exploit
ATT&CKT1098.004 T1021.004 T1078
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • A sysadmin who legitimately holds an unrestricted key for the git account for maintenance/break-glass
  • Backup or automation tooling that SSHes as git to run rsync/tar (would spawn those tools, not a login shell)
  • Configuration management running a remote command as git
  • Monitoring agents invoking a shell health-check as the git user
Suppression filter
| where not (ProcessCommandLine has_any ("rsync","borg","/usr/bin/tar","gogs serv"))
Response runbook
1) TRIAGE: Grep ~git/.ssh/authorized_keys for any entry lacking the `command="...gogs serv key-N...",no-pty` forced-command wrapper; capture the source IP from sshd/auth logs and the child ProcessCommandLine. 2) CONTAIN: Kill the session, remove the offending key line, rotate the git account's credentials, and firewall SSH to management ranges only. 3) HUNT: Correlate the session start with UC1 authorized_keys write events and UC2 hook execution, enumerate commands run in the shell (history/auditd), and check for lateral movement or persistence dropped from that shell.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName =~ "sshd"
| where AccountName =~ "git"
| where FileName in~ ("bash","sh","dash","ash","zsh")
| where FileName != "gogs"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessCommandLine
| order by Timestamp desc
ImProcessCreate
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where ParentProcessName endswith "sshd" or ParentProcessName == "sshd"
| where ActorUsername == "git"
| where ActingProcessName has_any ("/bash","/sh","/dash","/ash","/zsh")
| where not (ActingProcessName has "gogs")
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ParentProcessName, ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s) Reconnaissance High
Match vulnerability scanner output to the CVE(s) named in the article.
ATT&CKT1190
Data sourcesVulnerabilities DeviceInfo DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
// TVM tables are daily snapshots — pin a 1-day window so the engine
// partitions on Timestamp instead of scanning the full retention.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2026-52811")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where Timestamp > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId, VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

// Sentinel has no native TVM table. If MDE→Sentinel streaming is enabled,
// `DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities` is available. Otherwise this UC has
// no Sentinel translation — track via Update / Heartbeat OS-version
// gaps as a coarse approximation.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2026-52811")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId,
          VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).

Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → [LLM] Gogs git rebase --exec argument injection in PR merge (CVE-2026-52806) · Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children · Article-specific behavioural hunt — [GHSA / CRITICAL] CVE-2026-52806: Gogs vulnerable to RCE via git rebase --exec a

Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking. → [LLM] Gogs post-exploitation shell payload: ${IFS} / base64-decode / pipe-to-shell in git tree
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage. → Remote service execution — PsExec / SMB lateral movement
Gogs git rebase --exec argument injection in PR merge (CVE-2026-52806) AIExploitation High
Hunts the smoking-gun exploitation of CVE-2026-52806: the Gogs server process invoking `git rebase` with an attacker-controlled `--exec=` flag (or `${IFS}` space-bypass token) supplied via a malicious PR base-branch name. Gogs never legitimately passes `--exec` to rebase, so a git rebase carrying that flag under a `gogs` parent is high-fidelity RCE. Rationale: Uses the exact exploit primitive from the advisory: `git rebase ... --exec=<cmd>` with `${IFS}` space-bypass, scoped to a `gogs` parent process which is the only legitimate caller of rebase in the Merge() code path — so any `--exec` there is injection, not developer activity. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-qf6p-p7ww-cwr9 • https://github.com/gogs/gogs/blob/v0.14.2/internal/database/pull.go#L282
ATT&CKT1190 T1059.004 T1068
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • A pentester or vulnerability scanner validating CVE-2026-52806 against the instance
  • A security researcher reproducing the PoC in a staging Gogs deployment
  • A custom Gogs fork or plugin that intentionally invokes rebase with hooks (rare)
  • Admin debugging the merge path manually while impersonating the gogs service user
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessCommandLine !has_any ("nessus","qualys","burpsuite","pentest")
Response runbook
1) Triage: read the full ProcessCommandLine to recover the injected command and decode any base64; pull Gogs web logs around the same second for the 500-error PR-merge request to identify the offending repo, PR, and authenticated username. 2) Contain: isolate the Gogs host, kill the git/sh descendant processes, suspend and reset the abusing user account plus rotate all Gogs DB credentials, API tokens, SSH keys and 2FA secrets (git user has filesystem access to every repo). 3) Hunt: enumerate child shells, curl/wget callbacks, files dropped in /tmp, cron/systemd persistence, and access to other repositories under REPOSITORY_ROOT.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where FileName =~ "git"
| where ProcessCommandLine has "rebase"
| where ProcessCommandLine has "--exec=" or ProcessCommandLine has "${IFS}"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName =~ "gogs"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
ImProcessCreate
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where ActingProcessName endswith "git"
| where ActingProcessCommandLine has "rebase"
| where ActingProcessCommandLine has "--exec=" or ActingProcessCommandLine has "${IFS}"
| where ParentProcessName endswith "gogs"
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ParentProcessName, ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

id: 2f0b7c1a-9d4e-4a6b-8c2f-1e7a6b3d5c90
status: experimental
description: Detects the Gogs server (gogs) spawning git rebase with an attacker-controlled --exec flag injected via a malicious pull-request base branch name, achieving server-side RCE.

Gogs post-exploitation shell payload: ${IFS} / base64-decode / pipe-to-shell in git tree AICommand & Control High
Hunts the payload stage of CVE-2026-52806 where git's `--exec` runs the injected command via `sh -c`, spawning download-and-execute or decode-and-execute shells. Keys on the `${IFS}` space-bypass token and `base64 -d | sh` / `curl|sh` patterns from the advisory, scoped to processes parented by git/sh/gogs. Rationale: The advisory shows the concrete payload shapes — `--exec=echo${IFS}<base64>|base64${IFS}-d|sh` and `curl ...|sh` — validated end-to-end with a wget fetch inside the Docker container; `${IFS}` in a command line on a Gogs host is a near-unique obfuscation IOC, and base64-decode/pipe-to-shell under a git parent is not legitimate Gogs behaviour. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-qf6p-p7ww-cwr9
ATT&CKT1059.004 T1140 T1105
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate git server-side hooks that shell out to helper scripts
  • CI/build tooling on the same host using base64 in scripts (should not be parented by gogs)
  • Container init scripts fetching config via curl during startup
  • Pentester replaying the PoC payload against the instance
Suppression filter
| where not (InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("/usr/share/gogs/scripts","docker-entrypoint") and ProcessCommandLine !has "${IFS}")
Response runbook
1) Triage: decode the base64/${IFS} command line to recover the true payload and any callback URL/host; confirm the parent chain traces to gogs→git. 2) Contain: isolate the host, block the callback domain/IP at egress, kill the shell and any dropped implant, and rotate Gogs secrets and the git service-user credentials. 3) Hunt: search DeviceNetworkEvents/NetworkConnectIP4 for outbound connections from git/sh descendants, look for new files under /tmp and REPOSITORY_ROOT, cron/systemd persistence, and repeat exploitation from the same user across other repos.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("git","sh","bash","dash","gogs")
| where ProcessCommandLine has "${IFS}"
    or (ProcessCommandLine has "base64" and ProcessCommandLine has_any ("-d","--decode"))
    or (ProcessCommandLine has_any ("curl","wget") and ProcessCommandLine has "sh")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, InitiatingProcessParentFileName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, FileName, ProcessCommandLine, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
ImProcessCreate
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("git","sh","bash","gogs")
| where ActingProcessCommandLine has "${IFS}"
    or (ActingProcessCommandLine has "base64" and ActingProcessCommandLine has "-d")
    or (ActingProcessCommandLine has_any ("curl","wget") and ActingProcessCommandLine has "sh")
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ParentProcessName, ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc
title: Gogs RCE Payload Execution via git --exec Shell (CVE-2026-52806)
id: 8b3c4d21-6e7f-4a10-9b5c-2d8e1f4a6c73
status: experimental
description: Detects download/decode-and-execute payloads spawned by git/sh under the Gogs process, using the ${IFS} space-bypass token or base64-decode-to-shell seen in CVE-2026-52806 exploitation.
references:

author: SOC Detection Engineering
date: 2026/07/07

  category: process_creation

            - '\paexec.exe'
            - '\csexec.exe'
            - '\remcom.exe'
            - '\smbexec.py'
    selection_wmic_remote:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\wmic.exe'
        CommandLine|contains:
            - '/node:'
    condition: selection_psexec or selection_wmic_remote
falsepositives:
    - Sysadmins running approved remote-management tooling — wrap with
      a known-admin-host watchlist
level: high
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where Processes.process_name IN ("psexec.exe","psexesvc.exe","paexec.exe","smbexec.py")
       OR (Processes.process_name="wmic.exe" AND Processes.process="*/node:*")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.process_name, Processes.process, Processes.parent_process_name
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where Processes.process_name IN ("psexec.exe","psexesvc.exe","paexec.exe","smbexec.py")
       OR (Processes.process_name="wmic.exe" AND Processes.process="*/node:*")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.process_name, Processes.process, Processes.parent_process_name
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`

Klue/Icarus: bulk Salesforce CRM record retrieval via connected app (Case/Contact/Account/Opportunity) AIActions on Objectives Medium
Hunts the high-volume automated read/export of Salesforce CRM objects performed by the compromised Klue OAuth integration during the June 2026 Icarus/UNC6395 campaign. Article-specific because it anchors on the exact CRM objects (Case, Contact, Account, Opportunity) that were mass-queried via SOQL/bulk export by an authorized third-party connected app rather than an interactive user. Rationale: Anchors on the exact CRM objects (Case/Contact/Account/Opportunity) that Mandiant/Google Cloud confirmed were bulk-SOQL-queried and exported by the abused OAuth connected app, plus a per-hour volume threshold that separates a productized SaaS connector's steady sync from an attacker's automated mass extraction. Cross-checked against: • https://www.csoonline.com/article/4187907/klue-breach-exposed-salesforce-crm-data-through-stolen-oauth-tokens.html • https://cloud.google.com/blog/topics/threat-intelligence/data-theft-salesforce-instances-via-salesloft-drift • https://techcommunity.microsoft.com/blog/microsoftthreatprotectionblog/protect-against-oauth-attacks-in-salesforce-with-microsoft-defender/4450584
ATT&CKT1119 T1213 T1567.002
Data sources
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Nightly/scheduled Klue competitive-intel sync legitimately reading Account/Opportunity records
  • Sales-ops bulk report exports or Salesforce Data Loader jobs run by admins
  • Data-warehouse ETL connectors (Fivetran, Informatica, Mulesoft) extracting CRM objects on a schedule
  • SaaS backup tooling (OwnBackup/Own, Spanning) performing full-object snapshots
  • Marketing/RevOps enrichment integrations doing periodic full-table pulls
Suppression filter
| where AccountObjectId !in ("<known-klue-integration-objectid-AFTER-revalidation>") and not (UserAgents has_any ("Salesforce Data Loader","OwnBackup","Fivetran","Informatica"))
Response runbook
1) TRIAGE: Identify the AccountObjectId/CONNECTED_APP_NAME driving the spike — confirm whether it is the Klue integration service account and compare the hour's RecordOps to the prior 30-day baseline for that identity. Pull the UserAgents and SrcIPs aggregated. 2) CONTAIN: If it is Klue (or any unexpected connected app) exceeding baseline, disable/revoke the connected app's OAuth authorization in Salesforce Setup > Connected Apps OAuth Usage and revoke its tokens; do not just block the IP. 3) HUNT: Pivot on the same AccountObjectId across all Salesforce ActionTypes for the last 14 days, look for python-requests/aiohttp user-agents (UC2) and first-seen source ISPs (UC3), and enumerate exactly which records were read for breach-notification scoping.
CloudAppEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where Application == "Salesforce"
| where ActionType has_any ("Api","ReportExport","Report exported","Bulk","Mass","Export","Read")
| where ObjectType in~ ("Case","Contact","Account","Opportunity") or isempty(ObjectType)
| summarize RecordOps = count(),
            DistinctRecords = dcount(ObjectId),
            ObjectsTouched = make_set(ObjectType, 10),
            FirstSeen = min(Timestamp), LastSeen = max(Timestamp),
            UserAgents = make_set(UserAgent, 10),
            SrcIPs = make_set(IPAddress, 15),
            ISPs = make_set(ISP, 8)
            by AccountDisplayName, AccountObjectId, AccountType, ApplicationId, bin(Timestamp, 1h)
| where RecordOps > 1000   // bulk automated pull; productized Klue sync runs far lower, attacker mass-export spikes
| order by RecordOps desc

WEB
sourcetype="sfdc:reportexport" OR sourcetype="sfdc:api" OR sourcetype="sfdc:restapi" OR sourcetype="sfdc:bulkapi"
| eval crm_object=coalesce(ENTITY_NAME, ENTITY)
| where (crm_object IN ("Case","Contact","Account","Opportunity")) OR (EVENT_TYPE IN ("ReportExport","BulkApi","BulkApiV2"))
| bin _time span=1h
| stats count AS RecordOps dc(crm_object) AS DistinctObjects values(crm_object) AS Objects values(USER_AGENT) AS UserAgents dc(CLIENT_IP) AS SrcIPCount values(CLIENT_IP) AS SrcIPs by _time, USER_NAME, USER_ID, CONNECTED_APP_NAME
| where RecordOps > 1000
| sort - RecordOps
Salesforce API access bearing python-requests/aiohttp automation user-agent (Icarus OAuth abuse) AIActions on Objectives High
Detects Salesforce API/bulk calls whose user-agent is a raw Python HTTP library (python-requests, aiohttp), which Mandiant attributed to the Icarus/UNC6395 exfiltration scripts in the Klue/Salesloft-Drift OAuth campaign. Article-specific because a productized SaaS connector like Klue does not present python-requests/aiohttp as its session user-agent — that fingerprint indicates the stolen token is being driven by an attacker's ad-hoc script. Rationale: The python-requests/aiohttp user-agent is a high-fidelity, second-source-confirmed (Mandiant/Google Cloud) fingerprint of the Icarus exfil scripts driving a stolen OAuth token, and it cleanly distinguishes scripted attacker access from a legitimate productized connector's normal session UA. Cross-checked against: • https://cloud.google.com/blog/topics/threat-intelligence/data-theft-salesforce-instances-via-salesloft-drift • https://www.rescana.com/post/salesloft-drift-oauth-token-breach-enables-salesforce-data-theft-in-unc6395-icarus-attack-campaign-august-2026
ATT&CKT1528 T1078.004 T1071.001
Data sources
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • In-house Python integration scripts legitimately calling the Salesforce REST/Bulk API with a default user-agent
  • CI/CD jobs or scheduled data pipelines built on requests/aiohttp pulling CRM data
  • QA/sandbox automation against a production org
  • A developer running an ad-hoc simple_salesforce/requests script for a one-off pull
  • Low-code/iPaaS connector that happens to surface a python-requests UA
Suppression filter
| where AccountObjectId !in ("<known-internal-python-integration-objectid>") and AccountType != "DeveloperSandbox"
Response runbook
1) TRIAGE: Determine which AccountObjectId/CONNECTED_APP the python-requests/aiohttp sessions authenticated as — if it is an OAuth connected-app/service identity (not a named developer), treat as high priority and pull the SrcIPs and Objects accessed. 2) CONTAIN: Revoke the connected app's OAuth tokens and disable the integration in Salesforce; rotate the integration credential/secret. 3) HUNT: Correlate the same SrcIPs/AccountObjectId against UC1 (bulk record volume) and UC3 (new source infra); if confirmed, enumerate every object/record touched by those sessions for notification scope and check for log-tampering (Setup Audit Trail gaps).
CloudAppEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where Application == "Salesforce"
| where UserAgent has_any ("python-requests","aiohttp","python-urllib","Python/3")
| summarize EventCount = count(),
            Actions = make_set(ActionType, 12),
            Objects = make_set(ObjectType, 12),
            FirstSeen = min(Timestamp), LastSeen = max(Timestamp),
            SrcIPs = make_set(IPAddress, 15),
            ISPs = make_set(ISP, 8),
            AnonProxy = make_set(IsAnonymousProxy, 3)
            by AccountDisplayName, AccountObjectId, AccountType, UserAgent
| order by EventCount desc

| sort - Hits
Salesforce connected-app OAuth access from first-seen ISP / anonymizing proxy (stolen-token reuse) AIExploitation Medium
Surfaces Salesforce activity for a connected-app/service identity arriving from a source ISP never previously associated with that identity, or flagged as an anonymizing proxy — the signature of an attacker replaying a harvested OAuth token from their own infrastructure rather than from Klue's. Article-specific because the breach hinged on a long-lived integration token being used from attacker-controlled (proxied) infrastructure. Rationale: Replays the core breach mechanic — a long-lived OAuth/service token used from new (often anonymized) infrastructure — by leftanti-joining today's Salesforce source ISPs per identity against a 30-day baseline and prioritizing anonymous-proxy hits, which catches token theft even when API calls themselves look legitimate. Cross-checked against: • https://www.csoonline.com/article/4187907/klue-breach-exposed-salesforce-crm-data-through-stolen-oauth-tokens.html • https://cloud.google.com/blog/topics/threat-intelligence/data-theft-salesforce-instances-via-salesloft-drift
ATT&CKT1078.004 T1528 T1090.003
Data sourcesAuthentication.Authentication
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Klue (or another connected app) legitimately changing its egress IP/ISP after a cloud-provider migration or scaling event
  • A traveling admin or service account routed through a new corporate VPN/egress point
  • Genuine new integration deployed in the last 24h presenting a never-before-seen ISP
  • Cloud-hosted ETL/backup tooling whose provider rotates outbound IP ranges
  • Identity using a privacy-VPN endpoint flagged as anonymous proxy but operated by an employee
Suppression filter
| where not (ISP in ("<known-klue-egress-isp>","<corp-vpn-isp>") and AnyAnonProxy == false)
Response runbook
1) TRIAGE: For each first-seen ISP/identity pair, confirm whether the AccountObjectId is a connected-app/service identity and whether the new ISP/anon-proxy is expected (check change tickets for new integrations or vendor IP changes). 2) CONTAIN: If unexplained — especially anonymous-proxy hits on the Klue integration identity — revoke the connected app's OAuth tokens, force token re-issuance, and rotate the integration secret. 3) HUNT: Join this identity+IP back to UC1 (bulk reads) and UC2 (python UAs) within the same window; if any correlate, scope all records accessed from those sessions and review the Setup Audit Trail for evidence of log clearing.
let Lookback = 30d;
let Baseline = CloudAppEvents
    | where Timestamp between (ago(Lookback) .. ago(1d))
    | where Application == "Salesforce"
    | summarize by AccountObjectId, ISP;
CloudAppEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where Application == "Salesforce"
| where isnotempty(AccountObjectId)
| join kind=leftanti Baseline on AccountObjectId, ISP
| summarize EventCount = count(),
            Actions = make_set(ActionType, 10),
            Objects = make_set(ObjectType, 10),
            FirstSeen = min(Timestamp), LastSeen = max(Timestamp),
            UserAgents = make_set(UserAgent, 8),
            AnyAnonProxy = max(tobool(IsAnonymousProxy))
            by AccountDisplayName, AccountObjectId, AccountType, IPAddress, ISP, CountryCode
| order by AnyAnonProxy desc, FirstSeen desc

   or NewProcessName endswith "\cmd.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\rundll32.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\regsvr32.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\mshta.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wscript.exe"

CISA KEV: CVE-2025-67038 — Lantronix EDS5000 Code Injection Vulnerability

CISA KEV
2026-06-23 1 use case 0 techniques 2 kill-chain phases detected

Lantronix EDS5000 contains a code injection vulnerability that could allow attackers to inject arbitrary OS commands into the username parameter. Injected commands are executed with root privileges. Vendor: Lantronix, Product: EDS5000. Federal patch due: 2026-06-26.
